The lignans, matairesinol and eudesmin, were highly enriched in RWA and BUS, respectively, forming distinct abundance clusters and expanding the list of marker compounds. These compounds were selected based on their distinctive heatmap clustering, high relative abundance, presence in at least two districts, and the lack of prior reports of antibacterial activity.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-16-00109-f009"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12942557_metabolites-16-00109-g009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/fa3b/12942557/8a10704d701b/metabolites-16-00109-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="502" width="797" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-16-00109-f009/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Shows heatmaps of the metabolite markers from both annotations with no reported antibacterial activity, with rows representing compounds; columns represent samples grouped by district. Colour intensity indicates relative abundance (normalised scale). (<strong>A</strong>) GNPS and (<strong>B</strong>) SIRIUS. The cyan boarder lines indicates the selected markers for in silico antibacterial activity.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot7dot2-metabolites-16-00109"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7.2. Molecular Docking Analysis</h4>
<p>Molecular docking predicts how a ligand fits in a receptor’s binding site to form a stable complex, estimating binding affinity with scoring functions and assessing interaction energies [<a href="#B30-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-metabolites-16-00109">30</a>]. In our study, we observed simulations that revealed distinct structural differences in how the marker compounds, namely reinutrin, isoswertisin-2″-acetate, querciturone, sandaracopimaric acid, tigliane/ingenane diterpenoid, matairesinol, and eudesmin, interact with various Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacterial targets. Reinutrin showed very high predicted antibacterial activity by extensively interacting with critical enzymatic residues in both <em>Streptococcus mutans</em> (3AIC) and <em>Klebsiella pneumoniae</em> (6T77). In <em>S. mutans</em>, it achieved a binding energy of −10.9 kcal/mol (<a href="#metabolites-16-00109-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), docking deeply into the glucansucrase (an enzyme that converts sucrose into glucans, driving dental plaque formation) active site and forming multiple hydrogen bonds with residues His587, Asn862, Asn914, Asp593, and Asn481 (<a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figure S4</a>), key for sucrose recognition, acceptor alignment, and glucan-chain growth.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/metabolites-16-00109-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>A similarly strong interaction was seen in <em>K. pneumoniae</em> (<a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figure S5</a>), with reinutrin showing a binding energy of −10.4 kcal/mol (<a href="#metabolites-16-00109-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). It formed multiple hydrogen bonds with Asp187, Lys155, Gly88, Asn86, Gly182, and Met188, supported by hydrophobic contacts with Thr186, Arg15, and Pro181. These residues define the enzyme’s substrate-recognition area, and their involvement suggests direct disruption of catalytic activity.</p>
<p>Querciturone, a flavonoid-3-<em>O</em>-glucuronide, also showed promising antibacterial activity predictions, with clear binding interactions (−9.6 kcal/mol) in the <em>E. coli</em> 1G27 active site (<a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figure S6</a>). The ligand binds in a compact, deeply embedded position, stabilised by a network of hydrogen bonds involving Ile44, Gln96, Cys90, Gly89, and Gly45, and additional reinforcement through hydrophobic interactions with Leu91, complemented by a salt bridge with His132 and a π–cation interaction with Arg97, which collectively enhance its overall binding affinity. Querciturone’s ability to interact with multiple anchoring residues across different protein targets explains its consistently high performance in docking assays, including a strong binding affinity with (3AIC) (−11.1 kcal/mol) as shown in <a href="#metabolites-16-00109-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>.</p></section><section id="sec3dot7dot3-metabolites-16-00109"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7.3. ADME Property Predictions and Drug-Likeness</h4>
<p>ADME profiling via ADMETLab 3.0 [<a href="#B41-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-metabolites-16-00109">41</a>] revealed diverse pharmacokinetic profiles for the studied phytochemicals, with positive traits and some limitations (<a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S5</a>). All predicted Caco-2 permeability values were low (−6.593 to −4.754), indicating limited passive intestinal absorption. Although sandaracopimaric acid and eudesmin had higher scores, they did not meet the thresholds for effective GI uptake, as evidenced by their low MDCK permeability. P-gp interactions were minor; only the tigliane/ingenane diterpenoid and eudesmin were predicted to inhibit P-gp, suggesting that active transport does not significantly improve absorption. Plasma protein binding was high (69–97%), reducing the free drug fraction. Bioavailability predictions showed compound-specific variability. Reinutrin, Isoswertisin-2″-acetate, Sandaracopimaric acid, and Eudesmin were predicted to have oral bioavailability above 20%, whereas hydrophilic flavonoids, such as querciturone, had lower values. Metabolism predictions were favourable, with all compounds stable in human liver microsomes and exhibiting minimal inhibition of key cytochrome P450 enzymes, mainly CYP1A2 and CYP3A4. CYP2C19 inhibition was predicted only for matairesinol and eudesmin. Clearance and half-life data indicated moderate elimination, consistent with compounds of intermediate lipophilicity and limited polar metabolism. The calculated Quantitative Estimate of Drug-likeness (QED) scores ranged from approximately 0.22, indicating poor drug-likeness, to around 0.77, indicating good drug-likeness. Compounds like sandaracopimaric acid, matairesinol, and eudesmin showed QED values toward the higher end of this spectrum, suggesting they possess more favourable physicochemical properties for drug development. The ADME results show that although the compounds have good metabolic stability, low CYP inhibition, and acceptable drug-like properties, their limited intestinal permeability and high plasma protein binding pose pharmacokinetic challenges for systemic antibacterial use.</p></section><section id="sec3dot7dot4-metabolites-16-00109"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7.4. Predicted Toxicological Profiles of the Candidate Compounds</h4>
<p>The toxicity assessment using the ProTox-II platform provided insights into the predicted safety of phytochemicals, including evaluations of acute toxicity, organ toxicity, genotoxicity, immunotoxicity, endocrine effects, and stress pathways. Several compounds ranged from low to moderate predicted toxicity (<a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary File csv3</a>). Reinutrin and querciturone had LD<sub>50</sub> values of 5000 mg/kg (Class 5), indicating low toxicity. Isoswertisin-2″-acetate had a lower LD<sub>50</sub> of 832 mg/kg (Class 4), suggesting moderate toxicity. Sandaracopimaric acid, had the highest tolerability with an LD<sub>50</sub> of 20,000 mg/kg (Class 6). The tigliane-ingenane diterpenoid with an LD<sub>50</sub> of about 50 mg/kg (Class 2) showed high toxicity and was unsuitable for further development. Organ toxicity predictions highlight concerns for specific compounds. Reinutrin has a 0.74 probability, indicating borderline nephrotoxicity risk. In contrast, isoswertisin-2″-acetate triggered predictions of nephrotoxicity (0.85), with elevated carcinogenicity (0.71), mutagenicity (0.78), and immunotoxicity (0.81), raising safety concerns. Querciturone exhibits low toxicity probabilities, with carcinogenicity at 0.63, mutagenicity at 0.90 (inactive), and hepatotoxicity at 0.79 (inactive), indicating a better safety profile. Endocrine disruption assessments indicate that all compounds are inactive at the androgen receptor, oestrogen receptor, aromatase, and PPAR-γ, suggesting minimal endocrine risks. Stress-response pathway predictions were optimistic: compounds showed inactivity in the Nrf2/ARE, HSE, and p53 pathways, with probabilities mostly above 0.85. Mitochondrial membrane potential disruption was unlikely; querciturone’s probability was 0.63, but still non-activating. Toxicity predictions show most compounds are safe early on. Reinutrin, querciturone, and sandaracopimaric acid are top due to their low toxicity and lack of genotoxicity, endocrine effects, or stress-activation effects. Conversely, isoswertisin-2″-acetate, with moderate acute toxicity and high risks of carcinogenicity, mutagenicity, and immunotoxicity, should be deprioritised.</p></section></section></section><section id="sec4-metabolites-16-00109"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p>From our HPLC-DAD chromatogram (<a href="#metabolites-16-00109-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>) and the overlay of the extracted ion chromatogram (EIC) from LC-MS analysis (<a href="#metabolites-16-00109-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>), we can clearly conclude that the elution patterns are unique among the propolis extracts. A comparable profile was observed earlier in the HPLC-DAD chemical profiling of propolis, with numerous compounds identified over a total elution time of 80 min, predominantly phenolic acids and flavonoids [<a href="#B42-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-metabolites-16-00109">42</a>]. This chromatogram shows that the gradient effectively separates compounds across a broad polarity range, confirming its suitability for detailed profiling, even LC-MS/MS analysis. This confirms the chromatographic uniqueness and compositional differences among propolis from various locations in Uganda, consistent with a recent study reporting similar patterns in volatile compounds in Ugandan propolis [<a href="#B19-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-16-00109">19</a>]. The densest cluster of precursor ions in the MS/MS scatter plot appears within <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 200–500, suggesting that low- to medium-mass phenolics like luteolin and flavonoid glycosides derived from quercetin and kaempferol could be predominant in the samples [<a href="#B43-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-16-00109">43</a>]. Few precursor ions were detected above <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 800, suggesting that high-mass constituents are not prone to extensive fragmentation.</p>
<p>The molecular networking results clearly show the propolis’ chemical diversity, organising compounds into related families. Seven key clusters reveal a diverse range of natural products, mainly terpenoids and polyphenols. Spectral library annotation (orange nodes) identifies representative compounds, aiding classification. Notably, flavonoid clusters (2, 4, 5, 7) confirm flavonoids and phenylpropanoids as core components, consistent with propolis’ polyphenol profile. Clusters for terpenoids (1), xanthones/isoflavones (3), and spermidine derivatives (6) indicate diverse plant sources. These findings demonstrate molecular networking’s ability to profile complex natural products, group metabolites, and guide the annotation of known and new compounds, supporting targeted isolation and further research [<a href="#B44-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-metabolites-16-00109">44</a>].</p>
<p>Beyond the well-established metabolites identified by SIRIUS, the detection of additional, structurally related flavonoid glycosides suggests the presence of previously unreported or rare glycosylated flavonoids in propolis, thereby enriching current understanding of its chemical diversity. The occurrence of rutin and reinutrin, previously found in ethanolic extracts of Portuguese [<a href="#B43-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-16-00109">43</a>], Moroccan [<a href="#B45-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-metabolites-16-00109">45</a>], and Turkish propolis [<a href="#B46-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-metabolites-16-00109">46</a>], highlights the widespread occurrence and chemotaxonomic significance of quercetin-based glycosides across different regions. The annotation of flavonoids with a C-glycosidic bond implies that, such as puerarin, could be due to the ability of honeybees to forage on plant species such as <em>Clematis</em> selectively [<a href="#B47-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-metabolites-16-00109">47</a>]. The method further revealed significant structural differences among flavonoid glucuronides, particularly in the position of glucuronic acid attachment. For example, metabolites such as querciturone (C<sub>21</sub>H<sub>18</sub>O<sub>13</sub>; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>) have glucuronyl groups bonded at the 3-<em>O</em> position, along with evidence of 7-<em>O</em>-glucuronidation in flavonoids. Importantly, querciturone has been reported previously in Portuguese propolis [<a href="#B43-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-16-00109">43</a>], suggesting it occurs across various propolis chemotypes. Moreover, sakuranetin (RT 13.54 min; C<sub>16</sub>H<sub>14</sub>O<sub>5</sub>; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 286.08 [M + H]<sup>+</sup>), previously reported in Brazilian green propolis, is known to originate from botanical sources such as <em>Prunus</em> spp. and <em>Artemisia</em> spp. This association supports the role of plant resins from these taxa in shaping propolis’s chemical profile [<a href="#B48-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-metabolites-16-00109">48</a>,<a href="#B49-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-metabolites-16-00109">49</a>,<a href="#B50-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-metabolites-16-00109">50</a>]. While 3-<em>O</em>-methylmyricetin has not been reported in propolis before, luteolin has been found in samples from regions such as Mexico and Turkey [<a href="#B51-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-metabolites-16-00109">51</a>,<a href="#B52-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-metabolites-16-00109">52</a>], indicating its widespread presence across various propolis types. The 2′-hydroxychalcone group was also present, with pinocembrin dimethyl ether (RT 31.99 min; C<sub>17</sub>H<sub>16</sub>O<sub>4</sub>; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 284.11) as an example, whose isomer (pinocembrin methyl ether) was reported in propolis from the United Kingdom [<a href="#B53-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-metabolites-16-00109">53</a>]. Although these broader classes of natural products have been reported in propolis from various regions, the specific compounds identified in our study have not been previously documented. Prenylated flavonoids are present in propolis from areas such as Japan (Okinawa) [<a href="#B54-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-metabolites-16-00109">54</a>], Taiwan [<a href="#B55-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-metabolites-16-00109">55</a>], Brazil [<a href="#B56-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-metabolites-16-00109">56</a>], the Solomon Islands [<a href="#B57-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-metabolites-16-00109">57</a>], and Thailand [<a href="#B58-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-metabolites-16-00109">58</a>]. Notably, Omani propolis contains prenylated flavonoids derived from plants such as <em>Azadirachta indica</em>, <em>Acacia nilotica</em>, and <em>Mangifera indica</em> [<a href="#B59-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-metabolites-16-00109">59</a>]. This distribution, which differs by source, highlights the ecological significance and chemotaxonomic diversity of prenylated flavonoids across various types of propolis worldwide.</p>
<p>The flavonols detected by the GNPS annotation technique, which have been reported in both Brazilian and Portuguese propolis [<a href="#B43-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-16-00109">43</a>,<a href="#B56-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-metabolites-16-00109">56</a>]. Additional well-matched flavonoids included myricetin (RT 7.96 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 319.04; 8 peaks; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>), reported at high concentrations in Polish propolis [<a href="#B60-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-metabolites-16-00109">60</a>], and astragalin (RT 9.62 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 449.12; 8 peaks; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>), which, to our knowledge, is reported here for the first time in propolis. Kaempferol (RT 6.43 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 493.10; 7 peaks; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>), previously identified in Brazilian propolis [<a href="#B61-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-metabolites-16-00109">61</a>], and galangin (RT 31.96 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 271.06; 7 peaks; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>), a recognised marker of poplar-type propolis from temperate regions such as Europe, South America, some parts of Africa (South Africa), China, among others, was also detected [<a href="#B62-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-metabolites-16-00109">62</a>,<a href="#B63-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-metabolites-16-00109">63</a>,<a href="#B64-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-metabolites-16-00109">64</a>,<a href="#B65-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-metabolites-16-00109">65</a>]. Tricetin and luteolin were reported in different bee products, such as European honey made from <em>Eucalyptus</em> species [<a href="#B66-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-metabolites-16-00109">66</a>], which might also be the source of Ugandan propolis, specifically from known species like <em>Eucalyptus camaldulensis</em> Dehn [<a href="#B19-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-16-00109">19</a>] and bee pollen (for only tricetin) from Brazilian <em>Mimosa gemmulata</em> pollen grains, a known composition of propolis [<a href="#B67-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-metabolites-16-00109">67</a>]. Recently, studies on ethanolic extracts of Thai and Turkish propolis identified vitexin and apigenin [<a href="#B68-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-metabolites-16-00109">68</a>,<a href="#B69-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-metabolites-16-00109">69</a>]. Notably, several other members of this subclass, with similarly high levels of shared fragment ions, are reported here for the first time in propolis, broadening the known chemical diversity of this flavonoid group. Most of the tentatively identified compounds in the flavanone subclass had more than seven shared fragment peaks, with the majority of them being new to the propolis chemical space; however, hesperidin (RT 9.61 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 611, 14 peaks, [M+H]<sup>+</sup>) has been majorly reported in Polish propolis [<a href="#B60-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-metabolites-16-00109">60</a>] and alpinetin (RT 24.59 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 271.10, 7 peaks, M + H) in Chinese poplar-type propolis [<a href="#B70-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-metabolites-16-00109">70</a>].</p>
<p>Furthermore, phenolic glycosides (3 features; RT 4.17–9.21 min; 354.10–492.15 Da) were identified, with biflorin and isobiflorin as the prominent representatives in the phenolic compound group, which was strongly annotated by SIRIUS. These compounds have been previously isolated from Cameroonian propolis [<a href="#B71-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-metabolites-16-00109">71</a>], suggesting that certain phenolic glycosides may be common to propolis from different geographical regions. Additionally, chlorogenic acid is widely documented in various propolis chemotypes, with numerous reports in water and ethanolic extracts of Brazilian green propolis [<a href="#B56-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-metabolites-16-00109">56</a>,<a href="#B72-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-metabolites-16-00109">72</a>,<a href="#B73-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-metabolites-16-00109">73</a>,<a href="#B74-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-metabolites-16-00109">74</a>] and Turkish samples [<a href="#B75-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-metabolites-16-00109">75</a>,<a href="#B76-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-metabolites-16-00109">76</a>], emphasising its frequent occurrence in propolis rich in caffeoylquinic acid derivatives. In GNPS, the phenolic amide <em>p</em>-coumaroylagmatine, has previously been isolated from <em>Albizzia julibrissin</em> Durazz [<a href="#B77-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-metabolites-16-00109">77</a>], suggesting a potential connection to the botanical resin bees visit. This observation is particularly relevant because beekeepers at multiple sampling sites reported similar species, <em>Albizia coriaria</em> Welw., <em>Albizia grandibracteata</em> Taub., <em>Albizia gummifera</em> (J.F. Gmel.) C.A. Sm., <em>Albizia lebbeck</em> (L.) Benth., and <em>Albizia zygia</em> (DC.) J.F. Macbr. as plant sources associated with propolis collection (see <a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>). This agreement between chemical signatures and ethnobotanical observations strengthens the evidence that <em>Albizia</em>-derived resins could meaningfully contribute to the chemical profile of Ugandan propolis.</p>
<p>In addition to diterpenoids, other subclasses annotated in GNPS comprised secogermacrane sesquiterpenoids, acyclic monoterpenoids, and a pentacyclic triterpene, collectively characteristic of non-polar resin components often linked to coniferous plant sources [<a href="#B19-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-16-00109">19</a>]. Diterpenic acids are well-known components of propolis; for instance, isopimaric acid has been found in both Brazilian and Mediterranean (Greek) propolis [<a href="#B44-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-metabolites-16-00109">44</a>,<a href="#B78-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-metabolites-16-00109">78</a>,<a href="#B79-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-metabolites-16-00109">79</a>,<a href="#B80-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-metabolites-16-00109">80</a>] and sandaracopimaric acid has been identified in samples from Saudi Arabia [<a href="#B81-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81-metabolites-16-00109">81</a>]. Beyond the main diterpenoid subclasses, the analysis detected sarcophytol A, a distinctive cembrane diterpenoid. Its presence might indicate contributions from stored propolis, where fermented pollen or aged resin fractions can introduce cembranoid compounds into the overall chemical profile [<a href="#B82-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82-metabolites-16-00109">82</a>]. The dataset also identified manool, a labdane-type diterpenoid previously found in both Tunisian and Brazilian propolis [<a href="#B83-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83-metabolites-16-00109">83</a>,<a href="#B84-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84-metabolites-16-00109">84</a>], implying that labdane diterpenes may be a common chemical feature across different geographic propolis types. The only triterpenoid found was uvaol, a pentacyclic triterpene also present in Brazilian propolis [<a href="#B85-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B85-metabolites-16-00109">85</a>], supporting its potential role as a marker of triterpenoid-rich plant resins that contribute to propolis formation. The tigliane-type diterpenoid, annotated only in SIRIUS, represents a new finding in the chemical space of propolis, thereby expanding its known diterpenoid profile. Compounds belonging to this class are well documented in <em>Euphorbia</em> species and <em>Croton</em> spp., both members of the Euphorbiaceae family [<a href="#B86-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B86-metabolites-16-00109">86</a>,<a href="#B87-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B87-metabolites-16-00109">87</a>]. Surprisingly, these species are similar to what beekeepers told us during our raw sample collection, which included <em>Euphorbia tirucalli</em> L. and <em>Croton macrobotrys</em> Baill. as resin-producing plants frequently visited by bees (see <a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>), suggesting their potential contribution to propolis diterpenoid content.</p>
<p>Smaller subclasses were also identified, including dibenzylbutyrolactone lignans, with two significant compounds noted. Matairesinol was recognised both by SIRIUS (RT 23.31 min; C<sub>20</sub>H<sub>22</sub>O<sub>6</sub>; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 358.14; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>) and GNPS (RT 7.66 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 359.15; 11 shared peaks; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>) and has been previously found in hydroalcoholic extracts of Brazilian propolis [<a href="#B79-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-metabolites-16-00109">79</a>]. The second lignan, eudesmin (RT 30.86 min; C<sub>22</sub>H<sub>26</sub>O<sub>6</sub>; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 386.17; [M + H]<sup>+</sup>), was also detected, as reported for Canary Islands (Spain) propolis [<a href="#B88-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B88-metabolites-16-00109">88</a>]. Furthermore, GNPS identified a furanoid lignan named lariciresinol (RT 14.45 min; <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 721.33; [2M + H]<sup>+</sup>), which has also been previously found in three bee products, i.e., honey, pollen, and propolis, from Brazilian stingless bees [<a href="#B89-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B89-metabolites-16-00109">89</a>].</p>
<p>The combined analysis of the SIRIUS and GNPS datasets using retention times, accurate masses, adduct behaviour, and class-level fragmentation matches shows that Ugandan ethanolic propolis extracts have a highly diverse and complex metabolome. The chemical profile is mainly composed of flavonoids, including both heavily glycosylated forms and classical aglycones, along with significant amounts of phenolic acids and their derivatives, as well as a variety of resin-derived terpenoids. These key groups are supplemented by lignans, alkaloids, megastigmanes, and several rare, specialised metabolites, which, although less abundant, broaden the profile’s structural diversity. This extensive chemical diversity highlights the multiple botanical sources of Ugandan propolis. It indicates the variety of plant resins collected by bees across the sampled districts, showcasing the ecological and chemotaxonomic complexity of this natural product.</p>
<p>PLS-DA plots (see <a href="#metabolites-16-00109-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>) consistently differentiate districts, confirming a clear geographical pattern in Ugandan propolis’ chemical signatures. This segregation of chemical compounds within these samples and annotations, which contributes to their similarities in cases of overlap and differences within samples with tight, isolated clusters, may be attributed to bees foraging on common botanical species across various locations [<a href="#B19-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-16-00109">19</a>]. Several resinous plant species documented in our sample collection were noted across different regions. For instance, beekeepers from districts in western Uganda (BUS, MBA, and RWA) mentioned <em>Hesperocyparis lusitanica</em> (Mill.) Bartel (Cupressaceae) and <em>Melaleuca citrina</em> (Curtis) Dum. Cours (Myrtaceae). <em>Erythrina abyssinica</em> Lam. ex DC (Fabaceae) was observed in MAS and NAK, while <em>Mangifera indica</em> L. (Anacardiaceae) and <em>Eucalyptus camaldulensis</em> Dehn. (Myrtaceae) were reported in nearly all locations, as detailed in <a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>. The VIP analyses (<a href="#metabolites-16-00109-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>) revealed that RWA is the most chemically distinct district in both datasets. Additional notable markers included a bis-glycosylated chromen-4-one (VIP 1.91), distinguishing KIB; sarcophytol A (VIP 1.90), linked to MAS; myricitrin (VIP 1.75), characteristic of ADJ, present in the Myrtaceae and Fabaceae families, has been shown to exhibit various pharmacological effects, such as antioxidant, anti-allergy, anti-inflammatory, neuroprotective, antibacterial, and wound healing activities [<a href="#B90-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B90-metabolites-16-00109">90</a>,<a href="#B91-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B91-metabolites-16-00109">91</a>,<a href="#B92-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B92-metabolites-16-00109">92</a>]. The agreement between the two annotation methods strengthens the reliability of the PLS-DA models and verifies the presence of distinct regional chemotypes in Ugandan propolis.</p>
<p>The combined GNPS–SIRIUS heatmap analysis (see <a href="#metabolites-16-00109-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>) reveals that Ugandan propolis splits into two primary chemical groups, rich in flavonoids and phenolic glycosides (ADJ, KIB, LIR, and NAK), which closely resemble the characteristic chemical profile of temperate-region propolis [<a href="#B43-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-16-00109">43</a>]. Interestingly, these districts do not have similar climates, suggesting that their grouping in the same chemical space may be due to comparable local beekeeping methods, especially the prevalence of growing or keeping resin- and nectar-producing plants, which affect the bees’ foraging options [<a href="#B14-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-metabolites-16-00109">14</a>]. The second group of districts (BUS, RWA, MBA, and KOT) primarily included diterpenoids and compounds derived from resin. This indicates a distinctive chemotype associated with tropical resin-producing plants, with MAS at the centre and all chemical groups shared among them. This type of propolis is usually found in tropical areas, especially across Africa [<a href="#B93-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93-metabolites-16-00109">93</a>]. It contains terpenes (such as monoterpenes, sesquiterpenes, diterpenes, and triterpenes), stilbenes, flavonoids, and diterpenic resin acids [<a href="#B19-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-16-00109">19</a>,<a href="#B93-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93-metabolites-16-00109">93</a>]. These results suggest that in tropical regions such as Uganda, propolis cannot be strictly categorised by geographic boundaries because of substantial overlap in resin-producing plants across different landscapes. The oPLSDA models further indicated that regional vegetation and ecological gradients play a significant role in shaping metabolite profiles [<a href="#B19-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-16-00109">19</a>]. The DSPC networks supported the prediction of plausible biosynthetic relationships among the annotated metabolites. In SIRIUS, the observed connectivity pattern positions hydroxycinnamic acids as early intermediates in the biosynthesis of downstream phenylpropanoid-derived metabolites, such as flavonoids, lignans, coumarins, stilbenes, and glycosylated derivatives [<a href="#B94-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B94-metabolites-16-00109">94</a>]. The GNPS DSPC network exhibited coordinated behaviour among terpenoids, phenolics, and flavonoids, aligning with their biosynthetic pathways, namely the mevalonate, shikimate, and phenylpropanoid pathways, respectively [<a href="#B56-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-metabolites-16-00109">56</a>].</p>
<p>Molecular docking results ranked the two flavonoids, renuitrin and querciturone, as the best-predicted antibacterial candidates. Moreover, reinutrin’s polar interactions were further supported by π–π stacking with Tyr916 and Tyr610, as well as a stabilising hydrophobic contact with Asp588, collectively securing the molecule within the catalytic channel. These non-polar/hydrophobic bonds were also observed in a recent in silico study on green prenylated phenylpropanoids from green propolis against <em>S. mutans</em> [<a href="#B30-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-metabolites-16-00109">30</a>]. Since glucansucrase is responsible for extracellular polysaccharide synthesis and biofilm formation [<a href="#B95-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B95-metabolites-16-00109">95</a>], the occupation of these catalytic residues suggests that reinutrin could interfere with biofilm-related virulence mechanisms in <em>S. mutans</em>. Similarly, in docking studies with the <em>K. pneumoniae</em> (6T77) protein, Thr186 was identified as one of the residues that form hydrogen bonds with a propolis flavonoid ligand, indicating its possible importance in ligand binding [<a href="#B96-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B96-metabolites-16-00109">96</a>]. The presence of high binding energies, hydrogen bonds, π–π stacking, and hydrophobic interactions across pathogens suggests that reinutrin targets key residues. This supports effective enzymatic inhibition and broad-spectrum antibacterial activity. On the other hand, these hydrogen-bond interactions observed in querciturone (<a href="#app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figure S6</a>) are crucial for ligand binding to the 1G27 enzyme [<a href="#B97-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B97-metabolites-16-00109">97</a>]. This suggests a potential mechanism to inhibit its catalytic activity and reduce bacterial viability, although direct experimental evidence of growth inhibition has not yet been confirmed. Furthermore, its structural characteristics are consistent with known antibacterial mechanisms of polyhydroxylated flavonoids, including inhibition of bacterial enzymes by binding to deep pockets, disruption of DNA-processing proteins through aromatic stacking, and possibly damage to membrane integrity due to its balanced hydrophobic and hydrophilic features [<a href="#B98-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B98-metabolites-16-00109">98</a>]. These qualities collectively make querciturone a promising antibacterial candidate with diverse modes of action, warranting further biochemical and microbiological investigation. The flavonoid glycoside (isoswertisin-2ʺ-acetate) showed only moderate binding strength, ranging from −6.3 to −10.1 kcal/mol (<a href="#metabolites-16-00109-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) across the proteins. This may be due to the compound’s structure, in which acetylating the sugar moiety reduces the number of hydrogen-bond donors and increases steric bulk [<a href="#B99-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B99-metabolites-16-00109">99</a>]. This limits the ligand to shallower binding modes and lowers the number of stabilising interactions compared to reinutrin and querciturone. The ADME predictions showed that none of the chemical markers were predicted to cross the blood–brain barrier, which is advantageous for systemic antibacterial agents, as CNS penetration is unnecessary and could raise toxicity risks [<a href="#B100-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100-metabolites-16-00109">100</a>]. Drug-likeness filters showed variability; many met Pfizer and Golden Triangle rules, but some, including querciturone and tigliane-ingenane diterpenoid, failed Lipinski criteria (e.g., molecular weight or H-bond counts slightly too high) [<a href="#B100-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100-metabolites-16-00109">100</a>,<a href="#B101-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B101-metabolites-16-00109">101</a>]. Although these compounds passed the ADME test, some candidates posed issues such as insufficient oral bioavailability. To improve this, strategies such as structural modifications, nanocarriers or non-oral methods, such as topical treatments, could help realise their antibacterial potential and support clinical development. The compounds were predicted to be safe, with toxicity values consistent with the general observation that many natural products exhibit lower toxicity than their synthetic analogues [<a href="#B102-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B102-metabolites-16-00109">102</a>]. Although immunotoxicity models in ProTox-II demonstrate only modest predictive accuracy [<a href="#B103-metabolites-16-00109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B103-metabolites-16-00109">103</a>], any immunosuppressive alerts would affect the inclusion of any lead compound (s) for consideration of an antibacterial agent.</p></section><section id="sec5-metabolites-16-00109"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusions</h2>
<p>This study presents the first detailed LC–HR–QTOF–MS-based metabolomic profile of Ugandan propolis, combining untargeted metabolomics, computational annotation, chemometric analysis, and in silico antibacterial testing. It moves beyond previous GC–MS studies of volatile compounds, greatly expanding the known chemical diversity. The metabolome comprises flavonoids, phenolic acids and their conjugates, lignans, megastigmanes, and many diterpenoids, many of which are newly reported in propolis. Multivariate analysis revealed geographic differences among samples from nine districts, showing two main patterns: flavonoid- and phenylpropanoid-rich profiles in the northern hemisphere, and diterpenoid-rich profiles in the southern hemisphere of Uganda. This indicates that ecological and botanical diversity influence the chemical makeup and highlights LC–MS-based metabolomics for chemotaxonomic and geographic differentiation of African propolis. We recommend further structural confirmation of the putatively annotated metabolites using NMR. Molecular docking identified lesser-known compounds, such as polyhydroxylated flavonoids (reinutrin and querciturone), as promising antibacterial candidates with strong predicted binding affinities and acceptable drug-like properties, highlighting the need for confirmation through in vitro assays. Overall, the framework supports targeted isolation, validation, pharmacology, standardisation, value addition, and sustainable use of Ugandan propolis as an antibacterial resource.</p></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>
<p>We thank the MS-UL MS Core Facility at Leipzig University’s Institute of Analytical Chemistry, Faculty of Chemistry, for providing access to their UHPLC–ESI-HR-QTOF equipment for our measurements. The authors also acknowledge assistance with molecular docking from Asiimire Bonitor and Akello Ruth Olila at the Department of Chemistry, Kyambogo University. Ivan Kahwa extends his gratitude to his field research assistants and beekeepers from the 27 locations for providing raw propolis samples and for showing him the botanical species from which bees collect resins during field walks.</p></section><section id="app1-metabolites-16-00109" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Materials</h2>
